---

The Four Parts That Have to Pl/ork Together


Communities tend to focus on the aspect of their project that was most interesting to the original founders, for example political types getting super into writing by-laws, or spiritual types just meditating all the time, or therapists processing each other, or people who like psychedelics discovering they’re all God together and never getting to the point of deciding who will put their name on the deed.

I’ve broken the model I propose down into the 4 parts shown in the above diagram. Note, Pl/ork means play + work. Calling therapy “inner work” forgets the inner child, who also wants to play. (Forgive the neologism. I hope pl/ork isn’t too corny. 🌽): 1) inner pl/ork (reparenting), 2) relational pl/ork (peer counseling especially), 3: medicine pl/ork (like loveyhuasca or iboga), and 4) the practical logistical foundation which is most of this article, since I have guides separately on the other parts. Obviously the details are complicated, and jealousy, children, money, land, God, and iboga don’t fit neatly into four boxes, but let’s go with it.

These four parts leak into one another almost immediately. Governance can end up slyly incorporating personal tensions like jealousy. Those who have positions with more control of shared resources will automatically be affected by that psychologically (power corrupts, as they say). Those who know medicine tend to have another type of god complex since they may make life or death decisions for people routinely. How folks choose to raise children can expose values the founders never discussed. And myriad other categories arise, of course.

And let's face it: food, housing, and logistics are not enough reason to bind whole lives together. That's more like a situationship. A community needs a shared sacred inner practice that gives the difficulty of our life together a deeper meaning. We help each otther heal. and choose love over fear. And we turn outward with a purpose, using the escuelita model to bring our healing to the world.


Founded on Freedom

Nothing suppressed, everything  processed. Each of us guides our own practice, and our practices weave the community into the quilt that keeps us all warm and safe. Whatever answers we find stay fluid and organic, and don't freeze into doctrine.

Speaking of dynamically weaving ourselves together, though, a community does need some stable agreements that they're not tweaking and revising every time somebody has a revelation in breathwork. But these agreements need to stay open to lived experience.

When agreements get frozen, religious communities tend to call the frozen version doctrine. Secular communities on the other hand often call it “the process,” which can become just as sacred.

To give an example of striking the balance: My contemplative practice is grounded in the early Buddhist suttas, taken critically. Most are treasure. Some seem like late additions. I consider the Abhidhamma a scholastic wrong turn. with a critical reading. Most are treasure, while some are obviously late additions (sometimes to the point of real absurdity). A living community should relate to its own teachings the same way: devoted enough to practice them and secure enough to disagree. in that same critical manner while staying devoted to following what they've decided on so far.

1. Inner pl/ork

Probably the most obvious type of pl/ork is reparenting. Here’s an example: you teach your inner child geography, while s/he teaches you the world is a cat!

Inner-child reparenting means learning to identify as the best inner adult, which is comprised of the three inner adult roles: Nurturer, Protector, and Guide. The newly developed adult reparents their newly recognized inner child. The child receives all that love, protection, and guidance that you give it, and in this process actually gains more freedom to remain alive inside you.

And it's built on somatic regulation first. Listening to the body's feelings before making a decision with the mind. And that actually symbolizes the adult/child relationship to me in some way as well.

2. Relational pl/ork

Hearthwork distributes counseling among peers: equal time, attention without interpretation or fixing, and then you switch.

The idea is that the capacity to be there for people when they need help isn’t located in one professional or guru, or the founder of the group that everyone emotionally depends on, but in the community as a whole.

But since we're merging peer therapy with community, we also need to make sure  that the person caring about you and listening to you shouldn't translate that information into administration over your life, the way a professional therapist might diagnose you as this or that and then suddenly you're ineligible for certain careers.  Although obviously that information can come out regardless when people are in close quarters. 

Another thing is knowing which problems are beyond what peers can handle and need outside or expert help. And another is to deal with jealousy and conflict while it's still between the people involved, before it gets put on the agenda (generally at the bottom of the agenda). Speaking of jealousy, check out my Romance Guide, since that's a topic each person & each couple first handles by themselves.

3. Medicine pl/ork

Community can reach new heights by carefully incorporating serious plant medicines with peer guidance rather than exalting any one person as a special shaman.

This doesn’t mean that a peer-led ceremony should be done without structure. Things like screening participants, making sure the sitters are sober, having a plan for dealing with emergencies, planning for integration, and other rules and boundaries need to be dealt with beforehand. While there may be some pure learning that could come from having several altered members figure out what rules and boundaries are needed on the spot, it’s not something I recommend.

4. The container

This is the part about membership, land, resources, governance, children, leaving, etc. 

Like my father, I’m aiming for communities with less and less money involved, since money is antagonistic to the spirit of community. If I have money, I can get whatever I want, regardless of whether anyone cares about my wellbeing. That's the hegemonic philosophy of life we are trying to overturn. I think the path there has three stages.

First there will be no money exchanged between members for basic community functions like food, housing, care, tools, education, transport etc. Members might have outside jobs, and they bring an agreed share of that income in. This is probably a good place to start if someone wants to create a community now and have it be realistic.

Later all wages and other income earned by the members from work outside the community, or from business inside the community, will be paid into the common purse of the community. No member accumulates private wealth while a member of the community. Some version of this is how things have been done in the Bruderhof community for generations, and my father lived there for 17 years. It is also done, in a somewhat different way, at Twin Oaks, and a number of other income-sharing communities. So a common purse isn’t just a theory.

Eventually, the community would also stop using money outside. People would meet their needs directly, or through exchange (be it trade, gift, work, obligation, or whatever). I couldn't find a well-documented modern community that has gone this far, and in fact, it may be impractical in some locations because of government requirements like taxes on land. So perhaps the practical end point here is a community that is nearly entirely moneyless, but has a small collective cash boundary on the outside.

None of this does away with scarcity or economic power. There are lots of areas where such power can be wielded: access to food storage, housing, tools, land, medicine, transportation, and the outside world’s currency, to name a few. Even in a moneyless community, someone can control what's scarce. Who decides how these things are distributed? What do members owe one another? What happens when someone wants to leave?

These are less exciting topics to discuss spiritually. It's rare to see someone come back from an ecstatic vision with news of how to revamp the community’s policies on provisional members, or how to divvy up assets with a departing member, or how to prevent one founder from having control over the land, or what to do when a child reports something serious. And if they do, it's still something that needs a sober thinking-through.

But no amount of gorgeous land will make up for the mess that is created when one person owns the land or has the final say about what will happen on it. And when members come on board faster than the group can support them, all the peer counseling in the world won't make up for it, any more than medicine makes up for the lack of a written exit agreement.

The first three parts are the inside of the container, and this container is what gives those a stable place to manifest.

If one of them gets skipped, it will need to be dealt with eventually, at high stress, usually after the group has invested land, labor, or other resources and everyone has an opinion on how things should be.

then section 12 i rewrote a bit, still passes pangram fully:

The Math of Absorption, and Who This Isn’t For
Many perfectly intelligent grownups would rather sit around for six hours talking about solar panels than talk about jealousy, God, death, childhood, money, sex, psychedelics, or somebody wanting to leave, which is what I ask people to do in this article.

This does not surprise me, since we are taught to put on a show of competence in modern society. We have a lot of incentives to hide ourselves in our lives and disappear behind our screens the second things get uncomfortable. We have been encouraged by our institutions to be narrow in our skills, and then they act surprised when nobody can sit through a conflict without appointing a subcommittee or blocking somebody.

So the question isn’t whether a person is “good enough” for community. Rather, whether the fit is right. Consider how much attention they currently have available for other people, how willing they are to examine their own reactions. And on the other hand, look at whether the group has enough free resource/capacity to support them without becoming a full-time caretaker operation.

Some people have engaged in long practices of contemplation or therapy, or have had very good luck in childhood; other people are just trying to survive some emergency in their life and need care more than responsibility. Most of us are all of these at different times in our lives.



In the model I’m imagining, some of the functions that are outsourced to therapists in modern society are distributed throughout the community. In other words, there’s a lot of peer counseling going on, people listening to each other, being there for each other and helping each other process ordinary emotional stuff.

That does not make the community infinitely absorbent like a Heavy Flow Maxi Pad.

I’m going to use the phrase “free resource,” which comes from Re-evaluation Counseling, to refer to the attention, steadiness, skill and practical capacity people have left over for others once they’ve dealt with enough of their own immediate stuff, and “need” to refer to the amount of help people need at a given time.

Everyone has a certain amount of both in different ratios.

My own need has exceeded my free resource at many points. Good Lord. During one stretch, my first wife tried to kill me while psychotic because she believed I was her sociopathic father. Then she took my son. Later she tried to kill my mother, while her pimp stalked my sister’s family until they moved to Canada.

I could go on and on, but the math is pretty clear by now. There wasn’t much of me left over for anybody else. So when I say someone might need to stabilize and do inner pl/ork before helping found a community, I’m not saying it from a mountaintop.

The ability to welcome somebody is partly a problem of resources, then, not compassion -- how many people can a group take in without hurting the existing members or the people they are taking in?

And that also brings us to a difficult catch-22 for fledgling groups. The need for new members in a small community is greater, but the ability of that community to absorb disruption is less. With only eight people in the community, someone still has to grow the food and fix whatever breaks. Somebody has to balance the books and watch the kids. And the water system has to keep working. The water well doesn’t care how beautiful the new member’s heart is.

So early on, members usually need to have more available resource than acute need, and some practical skills to contribute. They usually need to be willing to learn, too. Once there are 80 members, though, there's usually a lot more free resource to draw on.

I still don’t know how to keep this language of capacity from becoming a respectable way to exclude people who are disabled, poor, less articulate, maybe simply uncomfortable or unfamiliar with therapeutic language, or for whatever reason deemed inconvenient. Pretending capacity is unlimited won’t solve that. Any real process would need concrete criteria, review by someone who wasn’t part of the decision, and a way to revisit a “not now” decision on membership. Even then, the power to veto membership applications based on capacity remains dangerously tilted towards elitism.

But before you submit your application to the community, submit it to yourself. This community may not be a good fit for you right now if:

You have a need that far outstrips your free resource right now. This doesn’t mean you’re bad or doomed, it’s about timing; it just means you need to take a first step of finding support where you are and building up your resources. If you don't have a free resource calculator on hand, an easy way to tell is if you’re running from some crisis right now and mainly need a place to land, get treatment, housing, legal aid, etc. A founding group shouldn’t pretend it can give you those. 

You want the feeling of village life but don’t want to take on governance, maintenance, conflict, care, etc.

You’re not willing to look at yourself when jealousy or conflict comes up.

Any humane membership process should have ways of not flat-out rejecting people who fall into these categories, or anyone else it can’t take right now, like saying “later,” or “in another role,” or “with more support.”

# Founderism: The Four Questions

In every community there was somebody who took the initiative to get it started. Somebody found the land, wrote the first documents, taught the method, recruited the first members, or kept things going when everybody else lost interest.

That person ends up with real influence, and pretending they don't just makes that influence harder to look at. When that power fails to get distributed as the conditions ripen for that, it's called founderism.

I've seen founderism in many communes that I've visited, and I can see it playing out if I were to be a founder as well. I've got all these proposals and visions here in my guides, and so it would be very easy for me to become the person who decides whether or not everyone else “understood” them correctly, even while I wish to remain humble and open to the input of all involved.

A few things help keep that in check. One is to judge the method by results people can actually see. Will they see the members safely flourishing without being trapped? Can conflicts get resolved? Are the kids doing well once they grow up? When the method gets a prediction wrong, can the community say so and change course? And if the community is meant to spread, do new projects actually start up and last? Those questions seem more important to me than how big or old the commune gets, or how many decisions are made by consensus.

Allow for parallel implementations. Fork the method. If the community finds a way to alter Hearthwork and get better results, they've taught me something. If every change they make still needs the founder's “blessing”, then it's not really running in parallel, is it?


The questions so far have been about the method. These four are about the founder:

Can the community respectfully override the founder? Can the community replace the role of the founder? Can the community alter the vision of the founder? Can the community exist without the founder?

Then follow the practical power. Whose name is on the deed? Who's in charge of the housing and the food stores, the vehicles and tools, and any bank account outside the community? Who keeps the records? Who decides who gets admitted, and who picks the people who review the founder? Who controls access to the kids, or to medicine, and who can make a complaint go away? The org chart might look flat and horizontal, while the keys, passwords, titles and even just general reputation all point to one person.

Remember, it's not an override unless it can actually work on the person in question. If the founder selects the person to review the founder, holds the records, has title to the land, and can kick out complainers, then there's no real way for the community to override the founder, no matter how many meetings it holds.

Also watch for the daily signs of founderism. Increasing focus on the founder over time. Sense that disagreement is failure. Every bad result is blamed on lack of good implementation. Increasing “insider” knowledge while public accountability decreases. Results are never measured. Sense that former members become morally contaminated the moment they leave.

Presence of one of these alone isn't proof of abuse, but if several are present, they are worth paying attention to before the founder can explain how the situation is uniquely different in a spiritual way.

Ask these questions about any community, teacher or method. And ask them about me, if people end up living on land I buy.

While founderism is a problem in every community, it's especially so in communities which form naturally over time as I'm describing may happen in my case. In such cases, there's no formal community and no formal rules until people decide to come together to make them. It doesn't make sense to do that at first, if, say, I'm the only one there and I don't know who else will be there that I could trust. If there's one other person who comes to visit, it also doesn't make sense, so there's a critical mass point at which people decide "we were just living together like friends, but now we want to formalize the community."

If I could find a suitable commune to join, that would basically reverse this problem. I would then be the one on probation. But if we are all starting together from scratch, then we would all be at an equal probationary level with each other.

It's a bit like the process of getting married. At first, you visit your girlfriend or boyfriend when schedules align and each of you agrees. Over time as you get closer to marriage (which hopefully is not a fully unconscious process), you start making decisions together and considering sharing more and more resources. Once married, if you decide to divorce, you split up the resources, although that's also contentious depending on how long the marriage was for, whether one person feels tricked, etc, and unfortunately often ends up in court. Leaving a commune is the divorce half of it, where the shared resources have to get split up. When joining or forming a commune, we can hopefully be more conscious about the process and less romantic.

Friends who come to visit don't get written terms, though. That's not how anything works, and at first they don't even know if they'll stick around for a day or a week. There are implied terms, like be respectful and be honest, and those shouldn't need to be in writing. If someone is offered something like an internship or a probationary period, that would require written terms. Or if it naturally evolves and they bring up wanting something like that, it could be done.

22 no, founderism isn't my term, it's in the commune literature, and it's the problem of power staying in the hands of the founders longer than it should (i.e. now there are capable people to take over but democratization is not happening at an appropriate pace to match the capacity for it, due to a variety of reasons)

Joel, 2026-10-25 00:27 UTC (Africa/Nouakchott, UTC+00:00), word for word:

https://www.pangram.com/feed/954042b6-e624-4c7f-87d7-db15c547d5c8?utm_source=chrome_extension&utm_medium=extension&utm_campaign=extension_share

While founderism is a problem in every community, it's especially so in communities which form naturally over time as I'm describing may happen in my case. In such cases, there's no formal community and no formal rules until people decide to come together to make them. It doesn't make sense to do that at first, if, say, I'm the only one there and I don't know who else will be there that I could trust. If there's one other person who comes to visit, it also doesn't make sense, so there's a critical mass point at which people decide "we were just living together like friends, but now we want to formalize the community."

 If I could find a suitable commune to join, that would basically reverse this problem. I would then be the one on probation. But if we are all starting together from scratch, then we would all be at an equal probationary level with each other. 

It's a bit like the process of getting married. At first, you visit your girlfriend or boyfriend when schedules align and each of you agrees. Over time as you get closer to marriage (which hopefully is not a fully unconscious process), you start making decisions together and considering sharing more and more resources. Once married, if you decide to divorce, you split up the resources, although that's also contentious depending on how long the marriage was for, whether one person feels tricked, etc,  and unfortunately often ends up in court.  When joining or forming a commune, we can hopefully be more conscious about the process and less romantic. 

---

Every practice in this article can be turned against the people using it.

“Nothing suppressed, everything processed” can become a situation where someone in the group must tell all, even if they don’t want to. I have seen that risk in groups that use the ZEGG Forum practice. A group starts out inviting honesty and ends by up treating privacy as resistance. But privacy also can be resistance to the communal spirit, so there has to be a dynamic, living, always re-assessed balance between what you and the community owe each other, and what remains your own domain.

Peer counseling can become peer surveillance, even if the tone is warm. And when communal therapy processes are pre-reqs for people to get medicine or to be respected members, it can feel like the community is gatekeeping. Well, it is gatekeeping. Some people simply should not be let in the gate, at least not yet. And some people will resent that, and choose to leave, and that's fine. Some communities will be stricter gatekeepers, and others will be more lax. There are pros and cons to each extreme, and again, a living balance must be struck. 

It should go without saying that nobody has to take medicine to have equal respect in the community. But medicine is an optional part of the overall therapy process, and if a member is avoiding the therapy process and becoming hard to deal with, they will naturally not belong at a certain point. 
 
No single person or permanent group of people should handle grievances and have the power to decide who gets into or stays in the community. Each person should have a usable avenue for exit. Dissent is important for growth. That doesn't mean everything everyone says is equally valid, though. Sometimes it really is their wounded ego projection, and that should be acknowledged. But again, balance, because if complaints are too often or too broadly painted as projection, that's as much a failure, if not more so. 

There are some basic human rights that can’t be voted away from you by the majority in a community, unless it's a question of resource availability as a whole. If safety and the conditions for health can be provided, it should be provided. People should be able to maintain relationships with others outside the community, as long as those relationships don't interfere with the community as a whole (for example, you can't bring your boyfriend to live with you if he's not only not a member of the community, but also antagonistic towards it). 

If your human rights are unfairly stripped from you, even by a majority vote, then you can vote with your feet.

Joel, 2026-10-11 23:12 UTC (Africa/Nouakchott, UTC+0), answers to OWNER-QUESTIONS.md questions 11 to 16, verbatim:

11. a
12. well this can be tricky to spell everything out... let's say a grandmother is demonizing the commune. cutting contact with her could make things way worse. i think the commune needs to intervene and help people decide on how to communicate with outsiders when it's tricky and dangerous for everyone like this. that's not advice I or anyone else can give now, it's in the moment, context by context.  
13. first off, "decide the process before the name" doesn't make any sense and it sounds AI. fix that. you are misunderstanding tho, the bar is not higher. i'm saying they won't fit it in if they don't follow the processes of the community. and if that gets to the point of impossible to live with, they will be kicked out. but naerly always they will leave way before they get kicked out, if they don't fit in. Of course, we should specify that they will not be demonized for not trying to fit in, they will be offered compassion, but ultimately it is up to them if they want to fit in and follow the communal processes. And in some cases perhaps the community will make exceptions for some people, like if they are actually sick and need care, maybe kicking them out would be like a death sentence. it depends on so many factors, i can't decide anything, it's up to the people involved.

The Medicine Part - Yes, I'm Naming It
Most of the writing on communities hasn't caught up with psychedelics. They've gone from taboo toward regulated use really fast (licensed service centers for psilocybin started opening in Oregon in 2023, Colorado set up a system for regulating natural medicines and began licensing in 2025, and New Mexico enacted a Medical Psilocybin Act in 2025), and Australia has allowed authorized psychiatrists limited access for a list of conditions since 2023, for both psilocybin and MDMA.

That said, it's not a smooth ride or an inevitability. The US FDA issued a Complete Response Letter to the MDMA-assisted therapy application in 2024, where they did not approve it and requested more evidence. And the thing with Portugal is that many people will lazily say "they've legalized everything there" - but that's false, they've only decriminalized possessing small quantities for personal use. It's still a crime to traffic drugs.

All of which means the legality of psychedelics varies by country and by state. It varies by substance, too. And it depends on a group’s religious status and on what exactly it does. So if psychedelic medicine is going to be part of a community at all, that community should definitely get actual local legal advice.

Most intentional communities using psychedelics are still not public about it, due to possible trouble (even where the law allows some use) from parents worried about children, from neighbors imagining chaos, from insurance providers who might drop them, and from local officials who may not understand any of the distinctions.

I'm talking about it here because not to talk about it would be to falsify my model. Many of the people who read this article are already familiar with my writing about psychedelics, including my loveyhuasca/Haoma material. If this article is going to be honest, it has to talk about both the value and the risk.

There’s a serious case against having medicine as part of a community. Physical and psychological negative effects of ayahuasca were common in the Global Ayahuasca Survey, although severe effects were far less common and context mattered. And these medicines can open things up faster than a person (or their group) can deal with.

They do also attract people who are all for revelation and hate the follow-through, people who can collect 12 ceremonies and 6 origin stories and still can't say sorry to their housemate. Any community based mainly around the peak experience is going to find out sooner or later that the insight doesn't do the practical work for it. Sometimes the dishes need doing and a scared kid needs comforting.

In my experience there are real reasons to take psychedelics communally too - eg they can lower defensive barriers which otherwise dominate every serious conversation, and create strong bonds between members of the community who have been through the harder nights together. But there are also negatives that can come from taking a psychedelic - confusion, grandiosity, dependency, fouling up dynamics between members. Are you mature enough as a community to tell the good from the bad?

Community Is What Makes Psychedelics Pl/ork Best!
Part of it is integration: an insight needs somewhere to land. What happens when you come back from an amazing experience which gave you insight and clarity about your life, into the same old environment where nobody knows what you’ve been through and you’re faced with the same old cues? It can be super helpful to have gone through the experience in a community, where the people who held you through it can also notice what happens to you over the following months.

The deeper you go, the more alienated you can feel. After my original ayahuasca ego deaths at 25, I had trouble finding anyone in the physical world who seemed to have experienced anything similar. I couldn’t even find them at Buddhist monasteries despite all the preaching about enlightenment. That helped give me a God complex, a Savior complex, or both, which then attracted exactly the right people to show me my limits. 😂 In a community of people who’ve sat through the same nights, you aren’t the only one who’s been there, and someone can catch the God complex early.

Do you trust the people you're relying on for support? Support from people you trust is different from paid support, which is often paying people you don't know to look after you for a night. Sometimes they're great, but sometimes the person with the feather has only known you for 4 hours and already has a cosmology to explain everything you say.

In a community, your sober sitter may be someone who's known you for years, and you feel the difference between being watched and being held. This is especially important when doing iboga or other long and medically risky experiences. For those you need to be properly screened beforehand and monitored continuously.

Your peers can also reality check any downloads you may have had. Psychedelics can give you insight and they can give you convincing nonsense, all in the same bright shining light. A few friends who know your history may notice the difference between new understanding and… mom.

One of the benefits of community is rhythm, and many traditional medicinal practices occur within ceremonies that have a communal schedule. The shared schedule of ceremonies, rather than each person doing ceremonies when they want, can serve to limit compulsive use and provide for integration time.

There are the practical benefits: logistics are easier when it comes to things like having childcare arranged by the group that parents are comfortable leaving their kid(s) with, not needing to drive away from the ceremony, having someone bring water to someone or just sit quietly with them while they come back to normal consciousness, etc.

And then there will be people to play with! Raves often let strangers fast-track intimacy (getting to a level of intimacy that usually comes after building trust), but imagine doing ecstatic music & dance in a community that already knows each other (and each other’s inconvenient habits). 💃🕺

But I won’t idealize psychedelics. Some people and communities should avoid them. Where access is ahead of competence, people will become casualties of enthusiasm. Is your community humble enough to say no? To pause? Using the medicine takes that. It also takes humility to refer people out, and admit when an experience did harm.

My Ceremony Principles
No special shamans here. Traditional lineages usually disagree with me on this, and many ayahuasca and Bwiti communities believe the medicine should stay under trained lineage authority. I respect what those traditions have preserved, and I've learned some "dos & don't dos" from their sensible practices and less sensible practices. For example, although I do highly value loving kindness meditation, inner child reparenting, and nibbana meditation before ceremony, during, and at all times, I notice a lot of people want their ceremony to look and feel like some kind of abstract mix of indigenous rituals & props to make it feel more authentic and sacred than their everyday life. I understand the benefit of setting aside one time and place for sacred experiences (the book, "God is Red" comes to mind), although the shadow side to that is making the spiritual seem like another world apart from your regular life, which is sort of opposite to integration.

And while I do think it's valuable to have someone highly experienced in navigating the spirit worlds who is able to sense where you are in those and guide you, I also wouldn't trust many of these shamans to guide me, considering it's common to find that even the ones who can heal people very well may still be happy to send evil spirits to kill someone, if that hit job is lucrative. I also don't trust their medical intuition in general, considering the Huni Kuin shamans are famous for claiming that all other ayahuasca shamans are giving people cancer by using the knotted B. caapi vine (since the knots remind them of tumors). They're right though that modern folk do very often decide they're ready to lead ceremonies long before they're really trained and prepared. I'm sure I could still learn a lot from those traditions, and they will continue to develop their understandings as well.

My basic issue is with placing one charismatic person above everyone else’s direct experience. The facilitator on the retreat circuit may be wise. Or they may be someone who hasn’t been vetted by anybody, who isn’t accountable to anyone, maybe even a sexual predator, or financially dependent on people coming back to them for more feather-waving. And the more deeply someone is opening up, the more it costs not to personally know who's running their ceremony.

Even in a culture of peer medicine, there could still be an informal "shaman", someone everyone trusts and gradually stops questioning, even though nobody gave them the title. I don't know yet how to avoid this. Things that might help would be to have rotation of roles, and public accountability. Though neither of these seem like a complete solution.

Still, I want people to learn to guide themselves and care for each other, within the limits of their competence. I’ve written more about peer-held ceremonies, and about the best psychopath shaman I ever met.

Just because it's a peer-led ceremony doesn't mean it's ok to be casually irresponsible. In fact, since the authority is spread around, there needs to be more competence. Some things are a must: screening for contra-indications (both medical and psychiatric), reviewing medications and combinations, having the right kind of sober sitter present, and deciding before anybody takes anything what kind of situations would require professional help or emergency care.

Also, I am especially cautious about medicines and protocols that I see as more destabilizing and/or presenting more of a medical burden. This includes some uses of ketamine and MDMA and bufo. My reasons are given in those articles, respectively, since they need more space.

Using medicine is secondary to all of the other pl/ork that comes first: reparenting, developing somatic capacity, and learning to do peer counseling. The medicine can deepen an existing practice, but it can't be a pharmacological short cut to what takes several months of honest relationship with other people.

This pre-requisite also takes care of another issue in one shot: it weeds out the experience collectors.  If someone is unwilling to put in 6 months learning to listen without imposing their solution, it is unlikely they will come out of a ceremony more relationally mature, regardless of how many self-transforming fractalized machine elves told them they are the re-incarnation of King David & Elvis.😂

Integration is a term thrown around so much that I'm not sure if it still has meaning. But basically, it's your everyday life. The ceremony is supposed to open something up in you. Look at your life in the weeks after the ceremony and see how things have changed. That includes your relationships, behaviors, sleeping habits, your decision-making, and perhaps most importantly, how well you can deal with frustration without declaring a new spiritual emergency. 😂

All of the “supports” are important: preparation, nutrition, screening for drug interactions, being disciplined about the dose, and having a plan for dealing with emergencies. They matter more than ceremony aesthetics. My current safety material is at Altered States Triage.

Finally, there are the legal issues. These vary a lot, and they are not always well-defined. As a community, you need to write down what is and isn’t allowed, which activities are private (to the individual, to a group in the community, or to the community vs the outside), and what kinds of activities have been reviewed from a legal standpoint. “I don’t think anybody around here has ever heard of that” is information, but it’s not the same thing as a legal opinion.

After the Psychedelic Calling
image 9

A lot of people feel the urge to form a village after going through some serious psychedelic and/or mystical experiences. Finding their people and helping others get to whatever just opened up is part of the same urge.

I’ve felt that urge myself. The problem is that it can come with grandiosity, and I’ve felt that too. You come back believing love can heal anyone, and then you meet people who don’t want to change, people who sincerely want to change but keep choosing the opposite, and people in such a crisis that their need for help is more than your enthusiasm can hold.

That call may be real, but forming a community based on omnipotence is a terrible idea.

A group that begins in collective spiritual euphoria can recruit too quickly and promise too much, and only do the math on how much need it can absorb once several people already depend on it. The sections on capacity and on membership, later on in this article, are partly there to protect the original generosity from that first rush.

Once the euphoria wears off and people have a more sober view of the call they feel, it can become useful. A community can be a place for people to train up in the practices, and sometimes go off and start another experiment. In fact, if people are training there so they can help others build communities of their own, they’d be working towards forming a real alternative. That answers the reasonable objection that communes are just privileged escape pods, which gets double dipped with the objection that psychedelics are also priveleged escape pods (or, alternatively, as some say, "a vacation for the poor").

One of the benefits of community is rhythm, and many traditional medicinal practices occur within ceremonies that have a communal schedule. The shared schedule of ceremonies, rather than each person doing ceremonies when they want, can serve to limit compulsive use and provide for integration time.

There are the practical benefits: logistics are easier when it comes to things like having childcare arranged by the group that parents are comfortable leaving their kid(s) with, not needing to drive away from the ceremony, having someone bring water to someone or just sit quietly with them while they come back to normal consciousness, etc.

And then there will be people to play with! Raves often let strangers fast-track intimacy (getting to a level of intimacy that usually comes after building trust), but imagine doing ecstatic music & dance in a community that already knows each other (and each other’s inconvenient habits). 💃🕺

But I won’t idealize psychedelics. Some people and communities should avoid them. Where access is ahead of competence, people will become casualties of enthusiasm. Is your community humble enough to say no? To pause? Using the medicine takes that. It also takes humility to refer people out, and admit when an experience did harm.

## My Ceremony Principles

No special shamans here. Traditional lineages usually disagree with me on this, and many ayahuasca and Bwiti communities believe the medicine should stay under trained lineage authority. I respect what those traditions have preserved, and I've learned some "dos & don't dos" from their sensible practices and less sensible practices. For example, although I do highly value loving kindness meditation, inner child reparenting, and nibbana meditation before ceremony, during, and at all times, I notice a lot of people want their ceremony to look and feel like some kind of abstract mix of indigenous rituals & props to make it feel more authentic and sacred than their everyday life. I understand the benefit of setting aside one time and place for sacred experiences (the book, "God is Red" comes to mind), although the shadow side to that is making the spiritual seem like another world apart from your regular life, which is sort of opposite to integration.

And while I do think it's valuable to have someone highly experienced in navigating the spirit worlds who is able to sense where you are in those and guide you, I also wouldn't trust many of these shamans to guide me, considering it's common to find that even the ones who can heal people very well may still be happy to send evil spirits to kill someone, if that hit job is lucrative. I also don't trust their medical intuition in general, considering the Huni Kuin shamans are famous for claiming that all other ayahuasca shamans are giving people cancer by using the knotted B. caapi vine (since the knots remind them of tumors). They're right though that modern folk do very often decide they're ready to lead ceremonies long before they're really trained and prepared. I'm sure I could still learn a lot from those traditions, and they will continue to develop their understandings as well.

My basic issue is with placing one charismatic person above everyone else’s direct experience. The facilitator on the retreat circuit may be wise. Or they may be someone who hasn’t been vetted by anybody, who isn’t accountable to anyone, maybe even a sexual predator, or financially dependent on people coming back to them for more feather-waving. And the more deeply someone is opening up, the more it costs not to personally know who's running their ceremony.

Even in a culture of peer medicine, there could still be an informal "shaman", someone everyone trusts and gradually stops questioning, even though nobody gave them the title. I don't know yet how to avoid this. Things that might help would be to have rotation of roles, and public accountability. Though neither of these seem like a complete solution.

Still, I want people to learn to guide themselves and care for each other, within the limits of their competence. I’ve written more about [peer-held ceremonies](http://ceremonies.u-dont-exist.com/), and about [the best psychopath shaman I ever met](http://sidney.u-dont-exist.com/).

Just because it's a peer-led ceremony doesn't mean it's ok to be casually irresponsible. In fact, since the authority is spread around, there needs to be more competence. Some things are a must: screening for contraindications (both medical and psychiatric), reviewing medications and combinations, having the right kind of sober sitter present, and deciding before anybody takes anything what kind of situations would require professional help or emergency care.

Also, I am especially cautious about medicines and protocols that I see as more destabilizing and/or presenting more of a medical burden. This includes some uses of [ketamine and MDMA](http://mdmaket.u-dont-exist.com/) and [bufo](http://bufo.u-dont-exist.com/). My reasons are given in those articles, respectively, since they need more space.

Using medicine is secondary to all of the other pl/ork that comes first: reparenting, developing somatic capacity, and learning to do peer counseling. The medicine can deepen an existing practice, but it can't be a pharmacological short cut to what takes several months of honest relationship with other people.

This prerequisite also takes care of another issue in one shot: it weeds out the experience collectors.  If someone is unwilling to put in 6 months learning to listen without imposing their solution, it is unlikely they will come out of a ceremony more relationally mature, regardless of how many self-transforming fractalized machine elves told them they are the reincarnation of King David & Elvis.😂

Integration is a term thrown around so much that I'm not sure if it still has meaning. But basically, it's your everyday life. The ceremony is supposed to open something up in you. Look at your life in the weeks after the ceremony and see how things have changed. That includes your relationships, behaviors, sleeping habits, your decision-making, and perhaps most importantly, how well you can deal with frustration without declaring a new spiritual emergency. 😂

All of the “supports” are important: preparation, nutrition, screening for drug interactions, being disciplined about the dose, and having a plan for dealing with emergencies. They matter more than ceremony aesthetics. My current safety material is at [Altered States Triage](https://badtrips.u-dont-exist.com/).

Finally, there are the legal issues. These vary a lot, and they are not always well-defined. As a community, you need to write down what is and isn’t allowed, which activities are private (to the individual, to a group in the community, or to the community vs the outside), and what kinds of activities have been reviewed from a legal standpoint. “I don’t think anybody around here has ever heard of that” is information, but it’s not the same thing as a legal opinion.

## After the Psychedelic Calling

[image 9](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!5y74!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F0844bbea-ba0a-4ff0-8b62-4e46426dae51_1672x941.png)

A lot of people feel the urge to form a village after going through some serious psychedelic and/or mystical experiences. Finding their people and helping others get to whatever just opened up is part of the same urge.

I’ve felt that urge myself. The problem is that it can come with grandiosity, and I’ve felt that too. You come back believing love can heal anyone, and then you meet people who don’t want to change, people who sincerely want to change but keep choosing the opposite, and people in such a crisis that their need for help is more than your enthusiasm can hold.

That call may be real, but forming a community based on omnipotence is a terrible idea.

A group that begins in collective spiritual euphoria can recruit too quickly and promise too much, and only do the math on how much need it can absorb once several people already depend on it. The sections on capacity and on membership, later on in this article, are partly there to protect the original generosity from that first rush.

Once the euphoria wears off and people have a more sober view of the call they feel, it can become useful. A community can be a place for people to train up in the practices, and sometimes go off and start another experiment. In fact, if people are training there so they can help others build communities of their own, they’d be working towards forming a real alternative. That answers the reasonable objection that communes are just privileged escape pods, which gets double dipped with the objection that psychedelics are also privileged escape pods (or, alternatively, as some say, "a vacation for the poor").

What's going on at Tamera's Children's Place deserves direct study ~~rather than~~ instead of caricature. My own bias is still toward a system where kids interact with more people, without making the parent-child bond ideologically inconvenient. If the father is unknown, the whole community can do the fathering, but the biological mom's role should always be honored.

Protecting children is the duty of adults, but it requires them to help also. "The Boy Who Cried Wolf" is a didactic children's story which continues to be useful generation after generation. The Buddha even taught his son honesty as the most important first lesson. I agree. We must teach children truthfulness before there's a crisis. "Make believe" must be labeled as such. When adults lie to children (like Santa is real etc), that teaches them to misunderstand the difference between reality and make-believe.  Make sure kids understand what they say will be taken seriously if they are known to be honest. Don't let the teaching turn into interrogation or punishment when they're not sure.  A scared or confused kid may get mixed up telling what happened, and that's to be expected.

Sometimes we also need to protect people from the rare, but real threat posed by dangerous children. I looked through the research corpus for a well-documented case of a child in an intentional community who remained seriously dangerous to others. I was looking to find the reported conduct, the assessment, the intervention, the review, and what happened later. I couldn't find one. It's possible that records are private, or that cases were referred elsewhere. Whatever the reason, I can't claim communities already know how to handle the hardest cases on their own. In those cases, the child, the family, and the people at risk all need access to competent help and review, hopefully including non-intrusive help from outside the home community.

All of the community's design and implementation will ultimately be reviewed by the kids, once they're grown, and their review weighs more heavily than founders usually want it to. Adults can go on about their intentions forever, but those grown kids who lived through the system as it actually manifested might have another perspective.

If a community's kids never leave, it has no way of knowing whether they'd come back freely. So let them study, travel and make friends, encounter other ways of living, and disagree with the founders without being treated as traitors.

Amish young people, age 16, go through a 2-year period of freedom from parental control called Rumspringa. During this time they might travel outside, althoug they normally choose to stay in the community and begin acting as adults, including courtship. At the end of this period, they decide as adults whether to join the church, and roughly 85 percent or more of them end up joining. Pop culture makes Rumspringa seem like a wilder, more universal teenage free-for-all than it is, but the main point is that they're baptized as adults, which means belonging is a commitment they choose on their own.

Every childhood narrows the future. Most ordinary parents aren't going to give their kid a bunch of money at 18 and say, "Go see the world." They'll try to get them ready for some next step that can actually work, so they can become self-sufficient, and eventually take care of their aging parents. A poor or money-free community may have even less cash to offer, and basic survival may rightly come first.

Unlimited freedom isn't realistic, but it doesn't need to be artificially impossible for grown kids to leave. Kids in or outside of intentional communities all need to learn enough about how the money world outside operates that they aren't helpless in it. Like how to get a job, pay rent, read contracts, handle banks, get IDs, avoid getting scammed, and how to ask for help when they're unsure about any of that. And all that sounds amorphous if it's not structured into plausible next steps outside the community (school, an apprenticeship, work, family, another community).

Kids also need transferable skills and education. It helps if they have relationships with people outside the community. They should be able to access to their own records and documents. It would be great if the community could also provide resources to smooth the transition, like temporary housing, help with travel, etc.

No upbringing can provide perfect freedom, because path dependence is a reality, but belonging and freedom should go hand in hand.

The Grown Children Get the Final Review

Couples who live together, share the same culture and even the same bed still disagree about screens, vaccinations, schooling, religion, food, discipline and medical care. And that's just two people. Now think of twenty adults, all with different childhoods.

Once a kid is already living inside the disagreement, goodwill doesn't answer those questions. The community has to share enough principles about raising kids to offer them coherence, and leave parents enough room to remain parents.

My own childhood broke many mainstream rules about what children should see and do. I won’t unpack all of it here. I’m grateful that my mother nursed me until I was five, grateful my parents didn’t vaccinate me, and grateful for nearly all of the unusual world they gave me.

Me as a child, wearing the pen holder my dad made, ready to write my Final Review 😹

I mention vaccination specifically because it's one of the values I would absolutely need to share with people in a community, if I was going to raise children there. I'm not trying to design a village for everyone, only saying that communities need some common ground among the parents on children's health, enough that they can actually trust one another.

All of the community's design and implementation will ultimately be reviewed by the kids, once they're grown, and their review weighs more heavily than founders usually want it to. Adults can go on about their intentions forever, but those grown kids who lived through the system as it actually manifested might have another perspective.

I actually learned how to parent my son from his future review via his quantum jumping time machine, which he explained in this video:

A friend who reviewed this draft grew up in a Christian commune in Colorado where her father was a pastor. She calls her childhood "a treasure and a burden." A treasure because she belonged and everybody cared for everybody. A burden because of the strict religious life she never chose.

What she said is more useful than another promo video showing kids smiling and carrying vegetables.

Communal Parenting Adds On

One of the biggest reasons I think community matters for kids is Jean Liedloff's The Continuum Concept. It's one outsider's interpretation of the Ye'kuana, and she may have idealized what she saw, but it's still a powerful picture: the babies are carried along in ordinary adult life, the kids are surrounded by people of several ages and learn by taking part, and it doesn't fall on one exhausted parent to be a child's whole social world. While a nuclear household can imitate some of this, a real village can make it normal.

Experiments where kids sleep and live apart from their parents deserve special caution. Research on Israeli kibbutzim found that the practice of kids sleeping communally eventually ended for a mix of reasons: developmental findings, parents' preferences and social change. Kids can benefit from high-quality group care and multiple loving adults in their lives, but replacing a secure attachment to their parents is a different matter.

What's going on at Tamera's Children's Place deserves direct study instead of caricature. My own bias is still toward a system where kids interact with more people, without making the parent-child bond ideologically inconvenient. If the father is unknown, the whole community can do the fathering, but the biological mom's role should always be honored.

Children Need Freedom Inside Real Protection

African kids enjoying their childhood!

Kids also need their own territory, places where they can create their own society, invent games and work out ordinary disputes, without always being under the management of adults.

I got a taste of that at a Unitarian youth camp, and it's still one of the freest memories I have. ZEGG's Kinderhaus works from a similar intuition. Adults take care of safety on the outside, but kids still get a world of their own.

This could be badly misunderstood, so the safeguards need to be explicit. The adults still have to pay attention to what goes on in a children's territory. Older kids can't be allowed to quietly boss the younger ones around there, and secrecy can't be treated as independence.

I also reject the suburban panic that treats every warm relationship between a child and an unrelated adult as suspicious. It's good for kids to have a whole bunch of grownups who know them and enjoy them, who would notice when something changes, and who would act when something's wrong. Fear like that can destroy that gift and still not prevent abuse.

There are several parts to this protection:

Give children real agency. When kids say no, it counts. No grownup has a right to their affection, physical contact, obedience outside of legitimate safety needs, or private access to them.

Create a no-secrets rule. No grownup has a right to ask a kid to keep a secret from their mom or another trusted adult, whether it's about a gift, a game, a touch, a conversation or a relationship. A surprise isn't a secret, because at the end of a certain date it has to be told. But secrets meant to isolate a kid aren't allowed.

Protecting children is the duty of adults, but it requires them to help also. "The Boy Who Cried Wolf" is a didactic children's story which continues to be useful generation after generation. The Buddha even taught his son honesty as the most important first lesson. I agree. We must teach children truthfulness before there's a crisis. "Make believe" must be labeled as such. When adults lie to children (like Santa is real etc), that teaches them to misunderstand the difference between reality and make-believe. Make sure kids understand what they say will be taken seriously if they are known to be honest. Don't let the teaching turn into interrogation or punishment when they're not sure. A scared or confused kid may get mixed up telling what happened, and that's to be expected.

Hear the child even when the accused is someone people love. Communities fail here, because the adult may be charismatic, useful, rich, spiritually important, or everybody's friend. Decide on the response process ahead of time, before anyone's face is attached to it. Keep everyone safe first, right away, then make sure the accusation is investigated competently and fairly. Nobody's status buys silence.

Make sure kids have relationships and a reporting route outside the community. Grandparents, cousins, local friends, teachers and other trusted adults give them perspective on their life, and someone to talk to. Make sure there's at least one trusted adult, advocate or reporting route they can reach without going through the community's leadership. In 1984, Vermont authorities seized 112 kids in the Island Pond raid, but a court rejected the state's blanket request and returned them. State intervention can fail spectacularly, and enclosing kids completely in a community can fail too. Kids need more than one world.

Sometimes we also need to protect people from the rare, but real threat posed by dangerous children. I looked through the research corpus for a well-documented case of a child in an intentional community who remained seriously dangerous to others. I was looking to find the reported conduct, the assessment, the intervention, the review, and what happened later. I couldn't find one. It's possible that records are private, or that cases were referred elsewhere. Whatever the reason, I can't claim communities already know how to handle the hardest cases on their own. In those cases, the child, the family, and the people at risk all need access to competent help and review, hopefully including non-intrusive help from outside the home community.

The Door Must Open Outward at Adulthood

If a community's kids never leave, it has no way of knowing whether they'd come back freely. So let them study, travel and make friends, encounter other ways of living, and disagree with the founders without being treated as traitors.

Amish young people, age 16, go through a 2-year period of freedom from parental control called Rumspringa. During this time they might travel outside, although they normally choose to stay in the community and begin acting as adults, including courtship. At the end of this period, they decide as adults whether to join the church, and roughly 85 percent or more of them end up joining. Pop culture makes Rumspringa seem like a wilder, more universal teenage free-for-all than it is, but the main point is that they're baptized as adults, which means belonging is a commitment they choose on their own.

Every childhood narrows the future. Most ordinary parents aren't going to give their kid a bunch of money at 18 and say, "Go see the world." They'll try to get them ready for some next step that can actually work, so they can become self-sufficient, and eventually take care of their aging parents. A poor or money-free community may have even less cash to offer, and basic survival may rightly come first.

Unlimited freedom isn't realistic, but it doesn't need to be artificially impossible for grown kids to leave. Kids in or outside of intentional communities all need to learn enough about how the money world outside operates that they aren't helpless in it. Like how to get a job, pay rent, read contracts, handle banks, get IDs, avoid getting scammed, and how to ask for help when they're unsure about any of that. And all that sounds amorphous if it's not structured into plausible next steps outside the community (school, an apprenticeship, work, family, another community).

Kids also need transferable skills and education. It helps if they have relationships with people outside the community. They should be able to access their own records and documents. It would be great if the community could also provide resources to smooth the transition, like temporary housing, help with travel, etc.

No upbringing can provide perfect freedom, because path dependence is a reality, but belonging and freedom should go hand in hand.

If the father is unknown, the whole community can do the fathering, but the biological mom's role should always be honored.

Children Need Freedom Inside Real Protection

African kids enjoying their childhood!

Kids also need their own territory, places where they can create their own society, invent games and work out ordinary disputes, without always being under the management of adults.

I got a taste of that at a Unitarian youth camp, and it's still one of the freest memories I have. ZEGG's Kinderhaus works from a similar intuition. Adults take care of safety on the outside, but kids still get a world of their own.

This could be badly misunderstood, so the safeguards need to be explicit. The adults still have to pay attention to what goes on in a children's territory. Older kids can't be allowed to quietly boss the younger ones around there, and secrecy can't be treated as independence.

I also reject the suburban panic that treats every warm relationship between a child and an unrelated adult as suspicious. It's good for kids to have a whole bunch of grownups who know them and enjoy them, who would notice when something changes, and who would act when something's wrong. Fear like that can destroy that gift and still not prevent abuse.

There are several parts to this protection:

Give children real agency. When kids say no, it counts. No grownup has a right to their affection, physical contact, obedience outside of legitimate safety needs, or private access to them.

Create a no-secrets rule. No grownup has a right to ask a kid to keep a secret from their mom or another trusted adult, whether it's about a gift, a game, a touch, a conversation or a relationship. A surprise isn't a secret, because at the end of a certain date it has to be told. But secrets meant to isolate a kid aren't allowed.

Protecting children is the duty of adults, but it requires them to help also. "The Boy Who Cried Wolf" is a didactic children's story which continues to be useful generation after generation. The Buddha even taught his son honesty as the most important first lesson. I agree. We must teach children truthfulness before there's a crisis. "Make believe" must be labeled as such. When adults lie to children (like Santa is real etc), that teaches them to misunderstand the difference between reality and make-believe. Make sure kids understand what they say will be taken seriously if they are known to be honest. Don't let the teaching turn into interrogation or punishment when they're not sure. A scared or confused kid may get mixed up telling what happened, and that's to be expected.

Hear the child even when the accused is someone people love. Communities fail here, because the adult may be charismatic, useful, rich, spiritually important, or everybody's friend. Decide on the response process ahead of time, before anyone's face is attached to it. Keep everyone safe first, right away, then make sure the accusation is investigated competently and fairly. Nobody's status buys silence.

Make sure kids have relationships and a reporting route outside the community. Grandparents, cousins, local friends, teachers and other trusted adults give them perspective on their life, and someone to talk to. Make sure there's at least one trusted adult, advocate or reporting route they can reach without going through the community's leadership. In 1984, Vermont authorities seized 112 kids in the Island Pond raid, but a court rejected the state's blanket request and returned them. State intervention can fail spectacularly, and enclosing kids completely in a community can fail too. Kids need more than one world.

Sometimes we also need to protect people from the rare, but real threat posed by dangerous children. I looked through the research corpus for a well-documented case of a child in an intentional community who remained seriously dangerous to others. I was looking to find the reported conduct, the assessment, the intervention, the review, and what happened later. I couldn't find one. It's possible that records are private, or that cases were referred elsewhere. Whatever the reason, I can't claim communities already know how to handle the hardest cases on their own. In those cases, the child, the family, and the people at risk all need access to competent help and review, hopefully including non-intrusive help from outside the home community.

The Door Must Open Outward at Adulthood

If a community's kids never leave, it has no way of knowing whether they'd come back freely. So let them study, travel and make friends, encounter other ways of living, and disagree with the founders without being treated as traitors.

Amish young people, age 16, go through a 2-year period of freedom from parental control called Rumspringa. During this time they might travel outside, although they normally choose to stay in the community and begin acting as adults, including courtship. At the end of this period, they decide as adults whether to join the church, and roughly 85 percent or more of them end up joining. Pop culture makes Rumspringa seem like a wilder, more universal teenage free-for-all than it is, but the main point is that they're baptized as adults, which means belonging is a commitment they choose on their own.

Every childhood narrows the future. Most ordinary parents aren't going to give their kid a bunch of money at 18 and say, "Go see the world." They'll try to get them ready for some next step that can actually work, so they can become self-sufficient, and eventually take care of their aging parents. A poor or money-free community may have even less cash to offer, and basic survival may rightly come first.

Unlimited freedom isn't realistic, but it doesn't need to be artificially impossible for grown kids to leave. Kids in or outside of intentional communities all need to learn enough about how the money world outside operates that they aren't helpless in it. Like how to get a job, pay rent, read contracts, handle banks, get IDs, avoid getting scammed, and how to ask for help when they're unsure about any of that. And all that sounds amorphous if it's not structured into plausible next steps outside the community (school, an apprenticeship, work, family, another community).

Kids also need transferable skills and education. It helps if they have relationships with people outside the community. They should be able to access their own records and documents. It would be great if the community could also provide resources to smooth the transition, like temporary housing, help with travel, etc.

No upbringing can provide perfect freedom, because path dependence is a reality, but belonging and freedom should go hand in hand.

The Grown Children Get the Final Review

Couples who live together, share the same culture and even the same bed still split over screens, vaccinations, schooling, religion, food, discipline and medical care. And that's just two people. Now think of twenty adults, all with different childhoods.

Once a kid is already living inside the disagreement, goodwill doesn't answer those questions. The community has to share enough principles about raising kids to offer them coherence, and leave parents enough room to remain parents.

My own childhood broke many mainstream rules about what children should see and do. I won’t unpack all of it here. I’m grateful that my mother nursed me until I was five, grateful my parents didn’t vaccinate me, and grateful for nearly all of the unusual world they gave me.

Me as a child, wearing the pen holder my dad made, ready to write my Final Review 😹

I mention vaccination specifically because it's one of the values I would absolutely need to share with people in a community, if I was going to raise children there. I'm not trying to design a village for everyone, only saying that communities need some common ground among the parents on children's health, enough that they can actually trust one another.

All of the community's design and implementation will ultimately be reviewed by the kids, once they're grown, and their review weighs more heavily than founders usually want it to. Adults can go on about their intentions forever, but those grown kids who lived through the system as it actually manifested might have another perspective.

I actually learned how to parent my son from his future review via his quantum jumping time machine, which he explained in this video:

A friend who reviewed this draft grew up in a Christian commune in Colorado where her father was a pastor. She calls her childhood "a treasure and a burden." A treasure because she belonged and everybody cared for everybody. A burden because of the strict religious life she never chose.

What she said is more useful than another promo video showing kids smiling and carrying vegetables.

Communal Parenting Adds On

One of the biggest reasons I think community matters for kids is Jean Liedloff's The Continuum Concept. It's one outsider's interpretation of the Ye'kuana, and she may have idealized what she saw, but it's still a powerful picture: the babies are carried along in ordinary adult life, the kids are surrounded by people of several ages and learn by taking part, and it doesn't fall on one exhausted parent to be a child's whole social world. While a nuclear household can imitate some of this, a real village can make it normal.

Experiments where kids sleep and live apart from their parents deserve special caution. Research on Israeli kibbutzim found that the practice of kids sleeping communally eventually ended for a mix of reasons: developmental findings, parents' preferences and social change. Kids can benefit from high-quality group care and multiple loving adults in their lives, but replacing a secure attachment to their parents is a different matter.

What's going on at Tamera's Children's Place deserves direct study instead of caricature. My own bias is still toward a system where kids interact with more people, without making the parent-child bond ideologically inconvenient. If the father is unknown, the whole community can do the fathering, but the biological mom's role should always be honored.
